**Assistant — Turn 38**

Reviewed. Volume 12 does not simply prove Haruno correct about “codependency.” It makes her diagnosis powerfully persuasive, then identifies its limit:

> 「共依存は仕組みだ。気持ちじゃない。言い訳にはなっても、理由にはなってくれない。」
> “Codependency is a mechanism, not a feeling. It may provide an excuse, but it cannot provide a reason.”
> —p.353

The label can explain how the trio sustains itself:

-   Yukino transfers difficult choices to Hachiman and Yui.

-   Hachiman finds value in being needed.

-   Yui relies upon Yukino’s silence to preserve her own place beside Hachiman.


But the label cannot determine what any of them should do next. Refusing all help may respect autonomy—or become abandonment. Helping may express love—or satisfy the helper’s need to feel indispensable.

The volume’s central question is therefore:

> If neither intervention nor withdrawal is morally innocent, what gives Hachiman the right to act?

His provisional answer is not purity, necessity, or psychological health. It is a promise.

## Source and structure

The supplied PDF is a complete Japanese scan with a usable OCR layer. Page references use the printed pagination.

# Volume 12

## 1\. Yui already knows what a clear answer will cost

The opening interlude returns to the seaside immediately after Volume 11.

The three have finally agreed that an answer must be produced. Yet Yui’s internal voice reveals that she does not actually want the “genuine” if it means a cold, cruel truth that ends their shared life.

She understands the contradiction:

-   ambiguity is dishonest;

-   an answer is necessary;

-   but a real answer may exclude someone;

-   and she believes that person will probably be her.


The chapter title says the coming choice will be regretted. That applies not only to Hachiman’s eventual intervention but also to Yui’s decision to stop preventing the truth.

For Yui, honesty is not automatically liberating. It is the decision to permit a loss she has anticipated all along.

## 2\. The volume distinguishes temperature from warmth

After the aquarium, Hachiman buys drinks for the three of them. The cans possess literal heat, but he realizes that heat is not the same as warmth.

Warmth comes from another person:

-   a knee brushing his;

-   a hand nearly touching;

-   a body sharing the cold;

-   a relationship in which such contact carries meaning.


He senses that the particular warmth among the three may never exist in this form again.

This develops Volume 11’s winter imagery. Their clubroom, tea, outings, and physical proximity have created real warmth. The problem was never that their happiness was false. The problem is that they cannot preserve it indefinitely without deciding what the warmth means.

## 3\. The trio can narrate its past but not its future

The three reminisce about the Service Club’s history: Yui’s cookies, the summer camp, fireworks, the cultural festival, Kyoto, the election, Christmas, New Year, and their various requests.

These memories prove that their relationship existed. They are not retrospective inventions.

Yet the conversation is carefully selective. They name what was pleasurable while avoiding the conflicts attached to those events. More importantly, they can discuss the past and present but cannot speak about the future.

Hachiman describes the future as:

> 「不可視で不可知、不可解にして不可逆」
> “Invisible, unknowable, incomprehensible, and irreversible.”
> —pp.35–36

They may have watched the same moon, but that does not guarantee that they will arrive at the same answer.

Shared experience creates connection. It does not create identical desire.

## 4\. Yukino’s request is to be witnessed, not rescued

Yukino finally explains what she wants.

She once wanted to inherit her father’s political work. Because Haruno was presumed to be the successor, nobody seriously treated Yukino’s desire as an available future. Her mother made the meaningful decisions while describing Yukino as “free.” Lacking a basis for choice, Yukino followed Haruno.

She now wants to investigate that path and, if necessary, relinquish it consciously:

> 「今度は自分の意志でちゃんと決めるわ。誰かに言われたからとかではなく、ちゃんと自分で考えて納得して、……諦めたい」
> “This time I want to decide properly through my own will—not because someone told me to. I want to think it through, accept it, and then…give it up.”
> —p.49

Her request is carefully limited:

> 「あなたたちに、その最後を見届けてもらいたい」
> “I want the two of you to witness its conclusion.”
> —p.49

She does not ask Hachiman to negotiate with her family or construct an answer. She asks him and Yui to remain present while she acts.

This is the healthier structure anticipated by Volume 11:

1.  Yukino identifies the problem.

2.  Yukino formulates the request.

3.  The others agree to a defined role.

4.  Help no longer means taking authorship.


## 5\. Yukino’s independence is still contaminated by shame

Although Yukino’s request is genuine, the standard she applies to herself is troubling:

> 「私が自分でうまくできることを、証明したい」
> “I want to prove that I can succeed by myself.”
> —p.50

She does not merely want to choose. She wants to prove that she is not dependent.

That desire has been shaped by:

-   her mother’s treatment of freedom;

-   Haruno’s accusation that she lacks a self;

-   Hachiman’s insistence that Yukino solve “her own problem”;

-   and Yukino’s shame about having accepted help.


She therefore risks replacing one imposed identity with another:

> “The dependent Yukino must become a person who needs nobody.”

Volume 12 repeatedly exposes this false binary. Authorship and assistance are not opposites. The question is whether help serves the chooser’s decision or replaces it.

## 6\. Yukino immediately demonstrates a healthier way to ask

At the apartment, Yukino tells Haruno that she intends to return home and speak with their mother. Haruno agrees to cooperate, although she initially reveals that she expected Yukino to discuss the romantic triangle instead.

Yui offers to help Yukino pack. Yukino initially hesitates, then asks directly:

> 「お願い、してもいいかしら」
> “May I ask you to?”
> —p.77

This small exchange matters. Yukino’s development does not consist of rejecting all assistance. She:

-   knows what she is doing;

-   decides what help would be useful;

-   asks for that help;

-   and remains responsible for the larger choice.


Yui’s assistance does not compromise Yukino’s autonomy because Yukino authors its purpose and limits.

## 7\. Haruno cannot become intoxicated

While walking with Hachiman, Haruno admits that alcohol does not allow her to surrender control:

> 「どんなにお酒飲んでも後ろに冷静な自分がいるの」
> “No matter how much I drink, there is always a sober version of me watching from behind.”
> —p.92

She can see her own face while laughing or making noise. Even her apparent loss of control feels like something happening to somebody else.

She predicts:

> 「君は酔えない」
> “You cannot become intoxicated either.”
> —p.93

This describes more than alcohol. Haruno and Hachiman cannot participate in emotion without simultaneously observing, interpreting, and suspecting themselves.

Haruno therefore envies and distrusts absorption. She wants something unperformed but cannot stop examining every performance for deception. Her provocations seek authenticity while making unselfconscious sincerity nearly impossible.

Her diagnosis of the trio is consequently insightful but interested. She is not a clinician outside the system. She is another person unable to ask directly for what she needs.

## 8\. The hidden photograph makes Yui’s knowledge explicit

While helping Yukino pack, Yui finds the photograph taken on the Destiny ride.

Hachiman and Yukino look surprised, silly, frightened, and happy. Yukino is curled behind Hachiman and gripping him tightly. Yukino has hidden the photograph in a black envelope behind a panda plush.

Yui restores it to the same place and pretends she never saw it.

She understands that Yukino has romantic feelings but intends to conceal them. She also recognizes why she does not confront Yukino:

> 「彼女の気持ちを聞くのはずるいことだ。
> 自分の気持ちを言うのはずるいことだ。
> でも、彼の気持ちを知るのが怖いから。
> 彼女のせいにしているのが一番ずるい。」
> “Asking about her feelings would be unfair.
> Stating my own feelings would be unfair.
> But I am afraid to learn his feelings.
> Blaming her for that is the most unfair thing of all.”
> —p.99

This clarifies Yui’s Volume 11 proposal. She was not only trying to protect Yukino. She hoped Yukino would continue suppressing her feelings, allowing the three-person relationship—and Yui’s proximity to Hachiman—to continue.

Yui is neither malicious nor purely self-sacrificing. She understands the system, benefits from it, and hates herself for benefiting from it.

## 9\. Komachi demonstrates that separation does not require disappearance

Komachi completes her entrance examinations and formally thanks Hachiman:

> 「お兄ちゃん、ありがとう。お世話になりました」
> “Thank you, Onii-chan. You’ve taken care of me.”
> —p.146

Hachiman cries because her gratitude contains both closeness and separation. Komachi is acknowledging that a phase of their relationship is ending.

But their bond survives.

This provides the volume’s clearest counterexample to the belief that autonomy requires cutting off help. Komachi can grow because:

-   Hachiman’s past care is named rather than denied;

-   her own competence is recognized;

-   their relationship is secure enough to change;

-   neither needs to pretend that the other was unnecessary.


Hachiman understands this as a gentle form of “graduating from one’s brother.” The problem is that he then maps the sibling model too easily onto Yukino.

Komachi is actually his sister. Yukino is not.

## 10\. The clubroom key represents authority over the relationship’s form

When Yukino is late, Hachiman tries to obtain the Service Club key. He realizes that Yukino has always carried it:

> He has never opened the clubroom himself. He has never even touched its key.

The symbolism is direct but not simple.

Yukino controls access to the institutional space. She makes the tea, opens the room, and supplies the recognizable form in which the trio meets. Hachiman and Yui have come to treat that form as natural, even though it depends upon her continuing to maintain it.

When Yukino chooses to run the prom outside official club activity, she effectively locks the old structure.

Hachiman accepts that lock almost immediately. He interprets withdrawal as the appropriate response to Yukino’s autonomy. But he does not ask whether the relationship can continue in another form.

Respect for a boundary becomes indistinguishable from waiting to be excluded.

## 11\. The prom gives Yukino a project whose purpose she chooses

Iroha proposes replacing the conventional graduation celebration with a prom. Her reasons are characteristically mixed:

-   she wants an impressive event;

-   she wants to establish a precedent for her own graduation;

-   she likes its visual and social possibilities;

-   she wants to attempt something difficult before future administrators can dismiss it.


When Yukino asks whom the prom is for, Iroha answers:

> 「もちろん、わたしのためです！」
> “For me, of course!”
> —p.206

The answer delights Yukino.

Iroha does not need a morally purified motive. She wants the project and accepts responsibility for pursuing it. That is precisely the sort of self-authored desire Yukino is trying to develop.

Yukino therefore volunteers to lead the work personally. She tells Hachiman and Yui that they are not obligated to participate and explains:

> 「自分の力でやってみたい」
> “I want to try doing it through my own strength.”
> —p.208

Yui understands immediately. She promises not to interfere, but makes Yukino agree to ask if she genuinely needs help.

That is a more nuanced agreement than Hachiman’s. Yui distinguishes giving space from becoming unavailable.

## 12\. Hachiman mistakes nonparticipation for successful individuation

After Yukino closes the clubroom, Hachiman tells himself that this is what independence looks like: sad, admirable, and worthy of celebration.

But his narration is suspiciously self-consoling.

He has previously defined his actions as work:

-   someone makes a request;

-   a task appears;

-   he completes it;

-   his personal desire remains irrelevant.


Now no request exists, so he concludes that there is no reason to see Yukino.

This exposes how thoroughly “work” has mediated their relationship. Without the club, Hachiman believes they have no legitimate contact. He watches Yukino walk toward the student-council room but does not call out because he cannot identify the position from which he would speak.

His withdrawal respects her stated wish. It also protects him from asking the more frightening question:

> Do I want to remain involved even when I cannot call it work?

## 13\. Iroha escapes the trap by making a separate request

Iroha directly asks Hachiman to help with the prom.

He initially refuses because he believes changing his answer would disrespect Yukino’s attempt to act independently. Iroha recognizes that his resistance is not indifference. He is already monitoring Yukino’s workload and warning Iroha not to rely upon her too heavily.

Iroha calls him:

> 「過保護」
> “Overprotective.”
> —p.250

She then attacks his habit of treating girls as younger sisters. Calling someone a sister, she argues, places her in another category and avoids treating her as a woman with potentially romantic desire.

Her final maneuver is explicit:

> 「わたし、先輩の妹じゃないですからね」
> “I’m not your little sister, Senpai.”
> —p.255

Iroha’s request creates a legitimate route into the project without claiming that Yukino asked for his intervention. Hachiman can assist Iroha while leaving Yukino in charge.

This is why Iroha is the “strongest junior.” She does not solve the moral problem theoretically. She changes the relational configuration.

## 14\. Iroha also learns not to let Yukino’s competence become invisible labor

During the preparations, Iroha realizes that Yukino has quietly completed so many tasks that the others have begun taking her work for granted.

She admits that smooth progress made her careless and that, if the pattern continued, she might become completely reliant upon Yukino.

This corrects the simple idea that Yukino proves autonomy by doing everything herself. If every institutional problem flows toward the most capable person, her competence merely becomes a reason to exploit her.

Healthy collaboration requires that others:

-   notice what she is doing;

-   accept responsibility for their own portions;

-   monitor concentration of labor;

-   and intervene before competence becomes self-destruction.


Iroha responds constructively. She does not retreat in shame; she adjusts her behavior.

## 15\. The promotional shoot turns performance into a rehearsal for reality

The prom’s publicity is staged. The decorations are temporary, the clothes rented, the dancing recorded, and most participants are acting out an event that does not yet exist.

Yet the performance generates real experiences:

-   Yukino confidently leads the project.

-   Iroha makes mistakes and allows Yukino to guide her.

-   Hachiman and Yukino dress one another.

-   Yui prepares Hachiman’s appearance.

-   Hachiman and Yui dance.

-   Participants enjoy an event they originally treated as fiction.


This extends Volume 10.5’s argument about Iroha’s date. Performance and authenticity are not opposites. A staged form can create the conditions for previously unrealized feelings or relationships to become perceptible.

The video does not merely represent a possible prom. It begins producing the social acceptance that could make the prom real.

## 16\. Yukino’s “prom king” role shows a self outside the old triangle

Yukino wears a tailcoat, leads Iroha in a formal dance, and becomes the promotional film’s prom king.

The scene matters beyond visual comedy.

Yukino is:

-   competent without being cold;

-   commanding without imitating Haruno;

-   playful without losing dignity;

-   protective without erasing Iroha’s participation;

-   and socially magnetic without Hachiman mediating her relationship to others.


She guides Iroha through mistakes and receives a genuine smile in response.

This is evidence that Yukino can create relationships and lead projects outside the Service Club. Her growth is not merely a negative separation from Hachiman. It contains positive capabilities and enjoyment.

## 17\. Yukino places Hachiman and Yui together—but her motive remains unstated

For an additional shot, Yukino asks Hachiman and Yui to dance together.

Her stated reason is practical: the editors need more footage, and using volunteers already present avoids burdening someone else.

The narration senses that a piece is missing from the explanation. Yui asks whether the two of them are really acceptable, and Yukino calmly says they are.

It is tempting to interpret this as Yukino surrendering Hachiman to Yui. The text does not prove that. Several motives may coexist:

-   she genuinely needs usable footage;

-   she trusts them;

-   she is trying to create distance;

-   she is suppressing her feelings;

-   she believes this arrangement will help Yui;

-   or she is testing her ability to let go.


The ambiguity matters. Yukino is making decisions, but autonomy does not guarantee that those decisions are free from shame or self-denial.

## 18\. Hachiman and Yui’s dance remains protected by excuses

Yui takes Hachiman’s hand. They put their hands on one another’s shoulders and move without knowing the proper steps.

Nobody is watching closely. They are only performers. It is only promotional footage. The surrounding crowd is also dancing.

Every fact provides an excuse.

The emotional intimacy is nevertheless real. Hachiman notices that Yui alone is looking at him.

This restages Volume 11’s Valentine mechanism: indirect structure can create enough safety for action. But it still does not tell either participant what the action means.

They can dance because they do not have to call it a dance between them.

## 19\. Yui briefly inhabits a possible domestic future

Hachiman and Yui shop for Komachi’s entrance-examination gift. At IKEA they enter a staged one-person apartment.

The showroom is composed of real objects but is not a lived home. That makes it an exact physical metaphor for Yui’s imagined future: plausible, detailed, emotionally affecting, but not selected or inhabited.

They discuss university, careers, childhood ambitions, and domestic life. Yui says one of her childhood dreams was:

> 「お嫁さん、とかね」
> “To become a bride, I suppose.”
> —p.313

Hachiman deflects with a joke about becoming a househusband.

Yui receives genuine one-on-one time with him. But even this outing requires Komachi’s gift as its justification. Like the prom dance, the possible date remains protected by another purpose.

The scene is warm precisely because it could become a life. It is painful because neither person says whether they want that life with the other.

## 20\. Yukino’s mother does not argue in order to be persuaded

The prom opposition originates with a parent who objects to its allegedly unwholesome appearance, risks, and lack of “high-school appropriateness.” Yukino’s mother presents that objection to the school.

Some of her concerns are reasonable:

-   the promotional photographs were circulated without anticipating parental scrutiny;

-   the event may create safety and supervision problems;

-   students cannot accept every form of institutional liability;

-   minority objections should not be ignored merely because online reaction is positive.


The problem is her method. She moves fluidly among procedural, moral, emotional, and reputational arguments while never allowing the conclusion to become negotiable.

Hachiman realizes that she is not participating in a discussion. Every response will simply produce another justification for the same predetermined result.

Her gentleness is a form of strategic control. Because she never becomes visibly aggressive, resistance can be characterized as childish, unreasonable, or disrespectful.

## 21\. Yukino’s mother is also trapped in a role

Haruno later explains that their mother’s personal desire may have little relevance. She was approached because of the family’s political and community position, so she believes she must intervene.

This complicates the antagonism.

Yukino’s mother is not merely a controlling individual. She is performing the role of:

-   mother;

-   political wife;

-   community representative;

-   school parent;

-   and protector of family reputation.


She resembles Hachiman more than he would like. Both say that personal desire is irrelevant because a role, request, or task requires action.

The family’s coercive structure can therefore reproduce itself without anyone explicitly choosing it. Each person performs the duty expected by the next institution.

## 22\. Hiratsuka’s departure makes the volume’s endings concrete

The confrontation reveals that Hiratsuka is likely to be transferred.

Her departure has been foreshadowed by her clean desk, remarks about finishing work, and increased concern about whether the students can continue without her.

Hachiman is hurt that she did not tell him. Later, when she demands that he put his reason for acting into words, he effectively accuses her of the same concealment:

> If important things must be spoken, why did she hide her departure?

Hiratsuka apologizes and continues waiting for his answer.

The distinction is subtle. She also postpones painful truth, but she does not use that postponement to decide his future. Her final guidance is still directed toward helping him articulate his own reason.

## 23\. Yukino explicitly asks Hachiman not to help

After the parental confrontation, Haruno asks whether interfering with Yukino’s project would respect her agency. She frames intervention as assuming responsibility for Yukino’s future.

Yukino then tells Hachiman:

> 「あなたの力はもう借りないわ。……お願い。私にやらせて」
> “I won’t borrow your strength anymore… Please. Let me do this.”
> —p.338

She admits that she has depended upon both him and Yui and fears that she will continue deteriorating if she cannot complete something herself.

Hachiman objects that her description is wrong, but eventually agrees.

This is not merely Hachiman being pushed away. Yukino has made a clear request, and respecting it is ethically serious.

Yet the scene also shows how completely she has absorbed Haruno’s interpretation. She treats every past failure as evidence that accepting help is itself corrupting. Her language of “proving” herself remains punitive.

## 24\. Haruno finally names the relationship “codependency”

Haruno describes the three as ordinary young people who perform fixed roles whenever they gather.

She asks Hachiman what their relationship should be called. When he answers “a love triangle,” she laughs and supplies her own answer:

> 「共依存っていうのよ」
> “It’s called codependency.”
> —p.346

She then targets Hachiman’s hidden benefit:

> 「あの子に頼られるのって気持ちいいでしょ？」
> “It feels good when she relies on you, doesn’t it?”
> —p.346

The accusation works because it identifies something real. Hachiman has repeatedly disguised personal desire as:

-   work;

-   responsibility;

-   older-brother instinct;

-   repayment;

-   necessity;

-   or the unavoidable consequence of a request.


Being needed allows him to experience intimacy without admitting that he wants intimacy.

When Yukino does not request his help, he feels the loss not only of work but of identity.

## 25\. The diagnosis expands beyond Hachiman and Yukino

The final interlude reveals that Yui is also part of the dependency mechanism:

> 「彼女が彼に依存したみたいに、あたしは彼女に依存したの。」
> “Just as she depended on him, I depended on her.”
> —p.359

Yui depended upon Yukino to:

-   keep her feelings hidden;

-   avoid pursuing Hachiman;

-   preserve the club;

-   postpone an answer;

-   and carry responsibility for the triangle’s paralysis.


This is the volume’s most important expansion of Haruno’s accusation.

The structure is not a simple dyad in which Yukino depends on Hachiman and Hachiman enjoys rescuing her. Each person receives something from the others’ inability to act:

The trio’s affection is real. The structure through which they preserve it is mutually reinforcing.

## 26\. Haruno’s label is still not the final truth

Hachiman initially accepts “codependency” with overwhelming self-disgust. It explains too many features of his behavior too efficiently.

But when deciding whether to act, he recognizes its limit:

> 「共依存は仕組みだ。気持ちじゃない。」
> “Codependency is a mechanism, not a feeling.”
> —p.353

A structural diagnosis can explain why someone’s action feels rewarding. It cannot prove that the action contains no love, loyalty, promise, or genuine concern.

Almost any form of care provides psychological benefit to the caregiver. If receiving that benefit automatically invalidated care, no act of help could ever be sincere.

Haruno has identified a danger, not supplied a complete moral verdict.

## 27\. Haruno’s “ordinary girl” remark exposes Hachiman’s idealization

Before naming codependency, Haruno insists that Yukino is an ordinary girl:

-   she likes cats and cute things;

-   dislikes ghosts and heights;

-   worries about her identity;

-   and struggles like other people.


Hachiman cannot accept that description. He still experiences Yukino as exceptional: beautiful, capable, intellectually superior, and fundamentally different from ordinary classmates.

His idealization helps produce the dependency he condemns. By imagining Yukino as uniquely strong, he:

-   overlooks her ordinary needs;

-   turns her requests into special obligations;

-   treats her weakness as a crisis;

-   and interprets helping her as an extraordinary role only he can fulfill.


Calling her “ordinary” does not mean denying her abilities. It means allowing her mistakes, uncertainty, desire, and need for help without converting them into evidence that her entire self has collapsed.

## 28\. The real conflict is responsibility, not help

Haruno asks whether Hachiman can accept responsibility for what happens if he intervenes in Yukino’s chosen test.

The question frightens him because it transforms “helping” from a bounded task into participation in another person’s future.

Yet demanding total responsibility is also an impossible standard. No teenager—and no adult—can guarantee the consequences of becoming involved in someone else’s life.

The more workable distinction is narrower:

-   taking responsibility for another person’s entire future is impossible;

-   taking responsibility for one’s own promise and actions is necessary.


Hachiman cannot guarantee that saving the prom will make Yukino independent, happy, or romantically available. He can decide whether abandoning her project is consistent with what he has promised and what he wants.

## 29\. Hachiman finally finds a reason that is neither work nor diagnosis

When news arrives that the school intends to cancel the prom, Hachiman immediately begins calculating how to reverse the decision.

Hiratsuka tells him that Yukino deliberately kept the cancellation from him. She then asks whether he still has a reason to help.

He cycles through inadequate answers:

-   Service Club work;

-   sunk costs;

-   concern about Iroha;

-   practical necessity;

-   codependency;

-   his need to feel useful.


Hiratsuka waits until he says:

> 「いつか、助けるって約束したから」
> “Because I promised that someday I would help her.”
> —p.353

The answer embarrasses him because it is ordinary and emotionally transparent. It does not prove that his motives are pure.

It is nevertheless his reason.

A promise differs from his usual excuses because it acknowledges a continuing personal relationship. He is not helping merely because a current request mechanically created a task. He is choosing to treat a past commitment as binding.

## 30\. His decision remains ethically unresolved

Hachiman’s intervention should not be presented as an uncomplicated triumph.

Yukino explicitly asked him not to help. She also deliberately withheld the cancellation from him. Acting against those decisions risks repeating his old pattern: interpreting another person’s needs and overriding her stated wishes.

But nonintervention also has problems. The prom is Yukino’s chosen project, and its cancellation is not the natural consequence of her incompetence. It results from institutional power, parental influence, and procedural retreat.

Allowing that pressure to destroy the project would not necessarily prove her independence. It might merely prove that isolated individuals lose against stronger institutions.

The conflict is therefore not:

> dependence versus independence.

It is:

> How can Hachiman oppose the forces obstructing Yukino without taking the project—and her life—away from her?

Volume 12 ends before answering.

## 31\. Yui understands Hachiman’s choice before he fully explains it

When Hachiman tells Yui that he is going to Hiratsuka, Yui says that if he becomes involved, things will probably work out.

Then she cries.

She claims the tears come from relief. The final interlude reveals that this is a lie. She hides because she knows that if Hachiman sees her suffering, he may stay and rescue her instead.

Yui refuses to become “the pitiful girl” because pity would reactivate Hachiman’s savior role.

This is both admirable and tragic. She protects his choice by erasing the evidence of its cost.

## 32\. Yui no longer wants Hachiman to be her hero

Yui remembers that Hachiman has been her hero since he saved Sable and was injured.

But she says:

> Her “someday” has already ended.
> He has already saved her.

What she wants now is not another rescue:

> 「ヒーローじゃなくていいから、ただ傍にいて欲しかった。」
> “He didn’t have to be a hero. I only wanted him to stay beside me.”
> —p.358

She then reaches the harsher wish:

> 「ちゃんと傷つけて欲しかった。」
> “I wanted him to hurt me properly.”
> —p.358

Continued kindness without a choice suspends her between hope and anticipated rejection. Being “properly hurt” would mean receiving an answer that recognizes her desire rather than indefinitely preserving her as the kind friend.

This is why Hachiman’s rescue ethic is no longer sufficient. Yui does not need him to solve a problem. She needs him to respond to her as a person whose desire may conflict with his.

## 33\. The final tears expose the cost of Yui’s kindness

Yui tells herself repeatedly that she is glad the tears stopped:

-   otherwise Hachiman could not leave;

-   otherwise he would help her;

-   otherwise she would influence his decision through suffering.


But the interlude ends:

> 「涙が止まらなければよかった。」
> “I wish the tears hadn’t stopped.”
> —p.359

The contradiction separates her outward performance from her inward desire.

She wants to behave ethically. She also wants him to turn back.

Neither feeling cancels the other.

Like Iroha’s mixed motives, Yui’s restraint is simultaneously generous and selfish, brave and avoidant. The difference is that Yui now knows exactly what she is doing—and can no longer call it simple kindness.

# Character movement

# What Volume 12 changes in the series reading

1.  **Yukino’s request is limited and precise.** She asks Hachiman and Yui to witness her choice, not make it.

2.  **Independence is not the absence of help.** Yukino’s direct request for assistance with packing is healthier than either silent dependence or absolute isolation.

3.  **The desire to “prove” independence can arise from shame.** Yukino’s project is self-chosen, but Haruno’s accusation influences the standard by which she judges herself.

4.  **Hachiman’s withdrawal is morally ambiguous.** It respects Yukino’s stated boundary while allowing him to avoid acknowledging that he wants a relationship outside Service Club work.

5.  **The clubroom key exposes the old relationship’s structure.** Yukino maintained the space; Hachiman and Yui treated access to it as natural.

6.  **Iroha creates a third path.** A separate, direct request lets Hachiman participate without pretending Yukino asked him to take control.

7.  **Pseudo-familial language conceals romantic specificity.** Treating girls as younger sisters allows Hachiman to care for them while evading what their desires might mean.

8.  **Performance can produce reality.** The promotional shoot is staged, but its cooperation, pleasure, attraction, and social effects are genuine.

9.  **Yukino is capable of leadership outside Hachiman.** Her prom work demonstrates positive agency, not merely separation.

10.  **Yui’s imagined future is increasingly concrete.** The apartment showroom and bride conversation make domestic desire visible, though still protected by jokes and another person’s errand.

11.  **Yukino’s mother is not merely irrational.** She raises real concerns but uses them within a discussion whose outcome she has already fixed.

12.  **Assigned roles can control the person performing them.** Even Yukino’s mother acts because her political and familial position supposedly requires it.

13.  **Haruno’s diagnosis identifies a mechanism, not the whole relationship.** Psychological benefit does not automatically invalidate love or care.

14.  **The dependency structure includes all three.** Yui depends upon Yukino’s silence just as Yukino depends upon the others’ answers and Hachiman depends upon being needed.

15.  **Idealization contributes to dependency.** Hachiman’s belief that Yukino is extraordinary prevents him from recognizing her ordinary needs and ordinary right to fail.

16.  **A promise becomes Hachiman’s first personal reason.** He acts because he said he would help, not because a formal task, debt, or role requires it.

17.  **His intervention may still violate Yukino’s wishes.** The next volume must distinguish helping her project from replacing her leadership.

18.  **Yui refuses to weaponize her pain.** She hides her tears because she knows Hachiman would respond to visible suffering.

19.  **That restraint is also another self-erasure.** She gives Hachiman freedom by denying herself the right to ask him to stay.

20.  **Yui wants recognition rather than rescue.** “Hurt me properly” means: answer my desire instead of preserving me through indefinite kindness.

21.  **Hiratsuka’s impending departure makes change unavoidable.** Even the adult who protected the club cannot maintain its form forever.

22.  **The romantic direction is now unmistakably asymmetrical.** Hachiman enjoys genuine intimacy with Yui, but when forced to choose a direction of action, he runs toward Yukino’s endangered project.

23.  **That is not yet a romantic confession.** His reason is a promise, and the ethics of acting against Yukino’s request remain unresolved.

24.  **The volume ends by splitting external and internal truth.** Hachiman runs toward action; Yui disappears from sight and cries where he cannot see her.


Volume 11 ended with Yukino claiming the right to formulate a request. Volume 12 tests what happens when everyone takes that request too literally.

Yukino tries to prove that she needs nobody. Hachiman treats withdrawal as respect. Yui promises not to interfere. Haruno gives the arrangement a diagnostic name.

Then institutional power threatens to erase Yukino’s choice entirely.

Hachiman finally rejects the idea that a diagnosis can decide the meaning of his feelings. He chooses to help because he promised—but he has not yet learned how to help without becoming the author of Yukino’s answer.

Meanwhile, Yui makes the opposite choice. She lets him go, hides the pain that might call him back, and refuses to remain merely the girl his kindness keeps from receiving an answer.

The prom has not yet been saved. The triangle has not yet been resolved.

What ends in Volume 12 is Hachiman’s ability to pretend that action can be justified without admitting whom he personally wants to reach.
